14.60 — Reidispongiolide A synthesis (footnote 1) — p.661 (p-18)

  • Citation: ref 1, Tetrahedron Lett. 2009, 50, 5012–5014.
  • Starting material: trityl glycidyl ether. The drawing puts a wedge on the ring C–O bond, which reads as (R)-2-[(trityloxy)methyl]oxirane (RDKit from a 2D wedge model; medium confidence). SMILES c1ccccc1C(c1ccccc1)(c1ccccc1)OC[C@H]1CO1
  • Sequence: 1) CH2=CHMgBr 2) H2O → A; 1) NaH 2) CH3I → B; cat. OsO4, NMO → C + D.
  • Asked: draw A, B, C, D.
  • Concept: Grignard attacks the less hindered epoxide CH2 (SN2, no change at the stereocenter); Williamson methylation; syn-dihydroxylation that gives two diastereomers.
  • Expected answer (high on constitution, medium on CIP):
  • A = (R)-1-(trityloxy)pent-4-en-2-ol C=CC[C@@H](O)COC(c1ccccc1)(c1ccccc1)c1ccccc1
  • B = (R)-2-methoxy-1-(trityloxy)pent-4-ene C=CC[C@@H](OC)COC(c1ccccc1)(c1ccccc1)c1ccccc1
  • C + D = the two diastereomers of 4-methoxy-5-(trityloxy)pentane-1,2-diol (new stereocenter at C2, configuration not controlled). SMILES OCC(O)C[C@@H](OC)COC(c1ccccc1)(c1ccccc1)c1ccccc1, giving (2R,4R) and (2S,4R).

14.61 — Fumonisin B1, C7–C9 installation (footnote 2) — p.661 (p-18)

  • Citation: ref 2, Tetrahedron Lett. 2012, 53, 3233–3236.
  • Context: fumonisin B1 is a potential sphingolipid biosynthesis inhibitor. To add C7–C9 in a recent synthesis, epoxide 1 was opened to give alkyne 2.
  • 1: cyclohexylidene-ketal (1,4-dioxaspiro[4.5]decane)–CH(OR)–CH2–(terminal epoxide, C5/C6). SMILES (no stereo) C1CCC2(CC1)OCC(O2)C(OR)CC1CO1
  • 2: …CH(OR)–CH2–CH(OH)(C5)–CH2(C6)–CH2(C7)–C8≡C9H. SMILES C1CCC2(CC1)OCC(O2)C(OR)CC(O)CCC#C
  • Asked: propose 1 → 2 starting from 3-bromo-1-propyne (propargyl bromide).
  • Concept: a carbon nucleophile opens the epoxide at the less substituted C6 (SN2).
  • Expected answer (medium): turn HC≡C–CH2Br into a propargyl organometallic (Mg → propargyl/allenyl-MgBr). It attacks C6 of 1, then H2O gives 2 (C6–C7 bond formed; C5–OH). The acidic alkyne H and propargyl/allenyl isomerism are real-world problems; the literature probably used a protected or modified version (not verified).

14.62 — (+)-Myrrhanol A (guggul component) (footnote 3) — p.661 (p-18)

  • Citation: ref 3, J. Org. Chem. 2009, 74, 6151–6156.
  • Context: guggul is the resin extract of the mukul myrrh tree, with potential effects on high blood pressure. The step comes from a recent synthesis of (+)-myrrhanol A.
  • 1: trans-decalin, labdane-type: 3-OTMS, gem-dimethyl, angular methyl, CH2OH on C9, and an exocyclic C8=CH2.
  • Reaction: 1) RCO3H (MCPBA); 2) LiAlH4; 3) H2O → 2 (C8 becomes a tertiary alcohol C(OH)CH3) as the only diastereomer.
  • Asked: mechanism of 1 → 2, and explain the stereochemistry at the new chiral center.
  • Expected answer (high): the peracid epoxidizes the exocyclic alkene from the less hindered face (concerted "butterfly"), giving a spiro epoxide. LiAlH4 hydride does SN2 at the less hindered epoxide CH2, giving a tertiary alkoxide; H3O+ protonates it. The C8–O bond is never broken, so the configuration of the C8–OH is set entirely by the face of epoxidation. That is why only one diastereomer forms.

14.63 — (S,S)-Reboxetine, Pfizer process (footnote 4) — pp.661–662 (p-18/p-19)

  • Citation: ref 4, Org. Proc. Res. & Devel. 2007, 11, 354–358.
  • Context: reboxetine mesylate is an antidepressant sold as a racemate. The (S,S)-enantiomer works against neuropathic pain. Pfizer route.
  • 1: (E)-cinnamyl alcohol OC/C=C/c1ccccc1
  • 1 → 2: t-BuOOH, Ti[OCH(CH3)2]4, (−)-DIPT (Sharpless). 2 + 2-ethoxyphenol, NaOH → 3 (drawn diol: ArO–CH(Ph)–CH(OH)–CH2OH). 3 → 4: 1) TMSCl, Et3N 2) MsCl, Et3N 3) H3O+. 4 + NaOH → 5 (terminal epoxide).
  • Asked: (a) structure of 2 ((−)-DIPT acts like (−)-DET); (b) mechanism 2 → 3 and the configuration at each chiral center of 3; (c) structure of 4 (TMS protects the less hindered 1° OH, MsCl mesylates the 2° OH, acid removes TMS); (d) mechanism 4 → 5.
  • Expected answers (RDKit CIP; high on mechanism, medium-high on absolute configuration):
  • 2 = (2R,3R)-3-phenylglycidol OC[C@H]1O[C@@H]1c1ccccc1. (+)-tartrate gives (2S,3S) with E-cinnamyl alcohol, so (−) gives (2R,3R).
  • 3: phenoxide does SN2 at the benzylic C3 with inversion → (2R,3S)-3-(2-ethoxyphenoxy)-3-phenylpropane-1,2-diol OC[C@@H](O)[C@@H](Oc2ccccc2OCC)c2ccccc2
  • 4 = 2° mesylate with a free 1° OH OC[C@@H](OS(C)(=O)=O)[C@@H](Oc2ccccc2OCC)c2ccccc2
  • 5: the 1° alkoxide does an intramolecular SN2 on C2–OMs (inversion) → (S)-2-[(S)-(2-ethoxyphenoxy)(phenyl)methyl]oxirane CCOc1ccccc1O[C@@H](c1ccccc1)[C@@H]1CO1. This matches the (S,S)-reboxetine target, which supports the assignment.

14.64 — 2-Methyl-D-erythritol (isoprenoid biosynthesis) (footnote 5) — p.662 (p-19)

  • Citation: ref 5, J. Org. Chem. 2002, 67, 4856–4859.
  • 1: (E)-4-(benzyloxy)-2-methylbut-2-en-1-ol OC/C(C)=C/COCc1ccccc1
  • 2 (as drawn): (2R,3R)-3-[(benzyloxy)methyl]-2-methyloxiranyl-methanol. CIP from a 2D wedge model in RDKit: C[C@]1(CO)O[C@@H]1COCc1ccccc1
  • 2 → 3: acetylation of the 1° OH (OAc). 3 + [H3O+] → 4, a bicyclic orthoester (1-methyl-2,6,7-trioxabicyclo[2.2.1]heptane carrying CH2OBn and a bridgehead CH3).
  • Asked: (a) reagents for 1 → 2; (b) mechanism 3 → 4 (hint: the ester C=O oxygen acts as the nucleophile).
  • Expected answer:
  • (a) (medium-high) Sharpless asymmetric epoxidation: t-BuOOH, Ti[OCH(CH3)2]4, (−)-DET (D-tartrate). By the same CIP mapping as (E)-2-hexenol / L-(+)-DET → (2S,3S), the (2R,3R) epoxide needs (−)-DET.
  • (b) (high) Protonate the epoxide O. The acetate carbonyl O attacks the tertiary epoxide carbon (the one carrying CH3 and CH2OAc). This is a 5-membered ring closure with SN1-like character, with inversion, and gives a 1,3-dioxolan-2-ylium cation. The freed C3–OH then adds to that cation carbon, and loss of H+ gives orthoester 4. Check: inversion at C2 and retention at C3 give, after hydrolysis, (2S,3R)-2-methylbutane-1,2,3,4-tetraol = 2-C-methyl-D-erythritol (RDKit). That agrees with the target.

14.65 — Corey–Chaykovsky epoxidation (footnote 6) — p.662 (p-19)

  • Citation: ref 6, Synth. Commun. 2003, 33, 2135–2143.
  • Reaction: trimethylsulfonium (1) C[S+](C)C + 1) NaH, 2) acetophenone CC(=O)c1ccccc1 → 2-methyl-2-phenyloxirane CC1(c2ccccc2)CO1 + dimethyl sulfide CSC.
  • Asked: mechanism.
  • Expected answer (high): NaH deprotonates a methyl to give the sulfur ylide (dimethylsulfonium methylide). The ylide carbon attacks the ketone C=O and gives a betaine alkoxide. The alkoxide then does an intramolecular SN2 on the CH2–S+Me2 carbon, with Me2S leaving, and closes the epoxide.

14.66 — Payne rearrangement / carbohydrate synthesis strategy (footnote 7) — p.662 (p-19)

  • Citation: ref 7, J. Am. Chem. Soc. 1982, 104, 3515–3516.
  • (a) Substrate: 2,3-epoxy alcohol carrying a 2,2-dimethyl-1,3-dioxolane (acetonide). With NaOH/H2O, PhSH it gives the acetonide–CH(OH)–CH(OH)–CH2SPh (1-phenylthio-2,3-diol). SMILES (no stereo) CC1(C)OCC(O1)C1OC1CO → CC1(C)OCC(O1)C(O)C(O)CSc1ccccc1
  • Asked (a): full mechanism with stereochemistry. The hint: NaOH makes an alkoxide that opens the epoxide intramolecularly and forms a new epoxide.
  • Expected (high): Payne rearrangement. The 1° alkoxide attacks C2 (inversion) and makes a terminal 1,2-epoxide plus a C3 alkoxide. This equilibrium is pulled forward because PhS⁻ opens the terminal epoxide at the unhindered CH2 (SN2). Net result: C2 inverted, C3 retained.
  • (b) Substrate: (E)-4-(benzhydryloxy)but-2-en-1-ol OC/C=C/COC(c1ccccc1)c1ccccc1 (drawn trans). Reagents: 1) t-BuOOH, Ti(OiPr)4, (+)-DET; 2) NaOH/H2O, PhSH → ?
  • Expected (medium): the (+)-DET epoxide is (2S,3S)-3-[(benzhydryloxy)methyl]oxiranyl-methanol OC[C@@H]1O[C@H]1COC(c1ccccc1)c1ccccc1. Payne rearrangement + PhS⁻ then gives (2S,3S)-4-(benzhydryloxy)-1-(phenylthio)butane-2,3-diol O[C@H](CSc1ccccc1)[C@@H](O)COC(c1ccccc1)c1ccccc1 (RDKit, inversion at C2).

14.67 — Laureatin skeletal rearrangement (footnote 8) — p.662 (p-19), structures p-19 right column

  • Citation: ref 8, J. Org. Chem. 2012, 77, 7883–7890.
  • Context: laureatin is a natural product from the red alga Laurencia nipponica with strong insecticidal activity against mosquitoes.
  • 1: a 2,4-disubstituted oxetane. C2 carries CH(OSiR3)–CH2–CH=CH–CH2CH3 (E); C4 carries CH(OH)–CH2CH2CH2–OR. SMILES (no stereo) CC/C=C/CC(O[Si](R)(R)R)C1CC(O1)C(O)CCCOR
    • Br2 → 2: a 2,3,5-trisubstituted tetrahydrofuran. It has CHBr–CH2CH3 on C2 (α to O), OSiR3 on a ring carbon, and CH2–(epoxide)–CH2CH2CH2OR on the other α carbon. Constitution as interpreted: CCC(Br)C1OC(CC2OC2CCCOR)CC1O[Si](R)(R)R. The OSiR3 position on the THF ring was read from a partial crop; confidence medium.
  • Asked: mechanism.
  • Expected (medium-high): Br2 forms a bromonium ion. The oxetane O attacks the proximal bromonium carbon (5-exo), giving a fused bicyclic oxonium (THF + oxetanium) with Br on the adjacent carbon (anti). The neighboring C–OH then attacks the oxetanium carbon C4 intramolecularly (inversion), opening the strained 4-membered ring and forming the epoxide. After loss of H+, the result is the THF + epoxide (oxetane → THF ring expansion).

14.68 — Oxymercuration of 1,2-epoxyalkenes (footnote 9) — p.662 (p-19)

  • Citation: ref 9, J. Org. Chem. 1981, 46, 930–939.
  • 1: 2-allyloxirane C=CCC1CO1 + 1) Hg(OAc)2, H2O 2) NaBH4 → 2 (96 %).
  • 3: 2-(but-3-enyl)oxirane C=CCCC1CO1 + Hg(OAc)2, H2O → [4] → 5 + 6 → NaBH4 → 7 + 8. Only a little normal hydration product forms.
  • 7 = 6-methyltetrahydro-2H-pyran-3-ol CC1CCC(O)CO1; 8 = (5-methyltetrahydrofuran-2-yl)methanol CC1CCC(CO)O1
  • Asked: (a) structure of 2; (b) structures of 4, 5, 6 and mechanisms 4 → 5 and 4 → 6; (c) why the product distributions for epoxyalkenes 1 and 3 differ.
  • Expected (high):
  • (a) 2 = Markovnikov hydration product, 1-(oxiran-2-yl)propan-2-ol CC(O)CC1CO1.
  • (b) 4 = bicyclic oxonium: the epoxide O has attacked the mercurinium ion at the internal carbon (5-exo), making a 1-oxonia-bicyclo[3.1.0] system with CH2HgOAc. Water opens 4 at the epoxide CH2 → 5 = 2-(hydroxymethyl)-5-(acetoxymercuriomethyl)THF (gives 8 after NaBH4). Water opens 4 at the epoxide CH → 6 = 6-(acetoxymercuriomethyl)tetrahydropyran-3-ol (gives 7). The ring-size outcome depends on which C–O bond of the oxonium breaks.
  • (c) In 3, the epoxide O can reach the mercurinium carbon through a favorable 5-membered transition state, so it out-competes water. In 1 that would need a strained 4-membered ring, so water attacks normally.

14.69 — Halonium-induced ether (alkoxy) transfer (footnote 10) — p.662–663 (p-19/p-20)

  • Citation: ref 10, J. Org. Chem. 2008, 73, 5592–5594.
  • Context: this electrophile-induced ether transfer was used to make several structurally related natural products.
  • Starting material: (S)- or (R)-4-(methoxymethoxy)-5-phenylpent-1-ene, PhCH2–CH(OCH2OCH3)–CH2–CH=CH2. SMILES C=CC[C@@H](Cc1ccccc1)OCOC (stereo as drawn, wedge; not CIP-assigned)
  • Reagents: 1) I2; 2) PhSH, Et3N.
  • Product: PhCH2–CH(OCH2SPh)–CH2–CH(OCH3)–CH2I, a 1,3-syn arrangement as drawn. SMILES (no stereo) ICC(OC)CC(Cc1ccccc1)OCSc1ccccc1
  • Asked: mechanism.
  • Expected (high): I2 forms an iodonium on the terminal alkene. The distal MOM oxygen (the OCH3 oxygen) attacks the internal iodonium carbon (6-membered TS) and gives a cyclic 1,3-dioxan-ium oxonium (anti to I). The ring then fragments at the O–CH2 bond, leaving OCH3 on the former alkene carbon and producing an oxocarbenium R–O=CH2+ on the original oxygen. PhSH/Et3N traps it to give the O–CH2–SPh (phenylthiomethyl ether). Net: OMe moves to C4, I goes to C5, and the 1,3-syn stereochemistry is set through the chair-like 6-membered ring.

14.70 — Artemisinin derivative epoxide opening with HFIP (footnote 11) — p.663 (p-20)

  • Citation: ref 11, J. Org. Chem. 2002, 67, 1253–1260.
  • Context: artemisinin (qinghaosu) is an endoperoxide from traditional Chinese medicine. Its synthetic derivatives are the standard treatment for Plasmodium falciparum malaria.
  • 1: the 9,10-epoxide of anhydrodihydroartemisinin. The epoxide is fused at C9 (quaternary, CH3) and C10 (CH, the anomeric carbon next to the ring O). The 1,2,4-trioxane endoperoxide is kept.
  • Reagents: (CF3)2CHOH (HFIP), cat. H3O+.
  • Product 2: 10-O-[CH(CF3)2] acetal at C10 and a tertiary OH at C9 (9-hydroxy-10-(hexafluoroisopropoxy) dihydroartemisinin). As drawn, OH and OCH(CF3)2 are cis (same face).
  • Asked: explain mechanistically why both the regiochemistry and the stereochemistry are opposite to what you'd expect (expected: attack at the more substituted 3° carbon, with anti, SN2-like inversion).
  • Expected (medium-high): protonating the epoxide breaks the C10–O bond to give an oxocarbenium ion at C10. The adjacent ring oxygen stabilizes it more than a tertiary carbocation at C9 would be stabilized, which is why C10 is attacked. The weakly nucleophilic HFIP then adds to the planar oxocarbenium from the less hindered face (or under anomeric/thermodynamic control). The C9–OH and the new acetal end up cis, i.e. net retention/syn rather than anti SN2 inversion.

Note: 14.25/14.26 (p-10), 14.27–14.59 have no literature footnotes. 14.50–14.53 are spectroscopy problems.

Klein 유기화학 — Chapters 15–19: literature-based problems

Source: 1d12609b-15-19.pdf (PDF page N = p-NN.jpg). Book page numbers given as printed. Confidence tags: [H] high, [M] medium, [L] low.

Chapter 15 — 적외선 분광법과 질량 분석법 (IR Spectroscopy and Mass Spectrometry)

Summary pages: book p.703–705 (PDF p1–3). Problems p.705–710 (PDF p3–8). References: book p.710 (PDF p8).

Key concepts (summary 15.1–15.16)

  • Light: wavelength/frequency/photon energy; vibrational excitation when photon energy = ΔE between vibrational levels.
  • IR: stretching & bending; wavenumber; diagnostic region (double/triple bonds, X–H) vs fingerprint region; frequency depends on bond strength and atomic masses; conjugated C=O absorbs lower than unconjugated.
  • Intensity depends on dipole moment change: C=O strong, C=C weak, symmetric C=C/C≡C silent.
  • Shape: H-bonded (concentrated) alcohol O–H broad; dilute alcohol narrow free O–H. 1° amines: symmetric + asymmetric N–H stretch (2 bands).
  • Analyzing IR: check 1600–1850 (C=O, C=C), 2100–2300 (C≡C, C≡N), 2750–4000 (X–H; line at 3000 cm⁻¹ separates sp² vs sp³ C–H).
  • MS: EI → radical cation M⁺•, fragmentation, m/z, base peak; nitrogen rule; (M+1) gives # C (÷1.1%); (M+2) 1/3 of M → Cl, equal → Br; M–15 (methyl loss), M–29 (ethyl loss); fragmentation favored for stable carbocations; HRMS gives molecular formula (exact masses); GC-MS (retention time); ESI for large biomolecules.
  • HDI (hydrogen deficiency index) / degrees of unsaturation: add H for halogen, ignore O, subtract H for N.

Literature-footnoted problems (refs 1–10)

15.69 — book p.708 — footnote 1

  • Citation: Anal. Chem. 2013, 85, 831–836.
  • Compound: anthraquinone acid dye (blue textile fiber dye) — 1-amino-4-(4-methoxyanilino)-9,10-dioxoanthracene-2-sulfonate (anion, as drawn). SMILES [H as drawn]: Nc1c(S(=O)(=O)[O-])cc(Nc2ccc(OC)cc2)c2C(=O)c3ccccc3C(=O)c12 — C21H15N2O6S⁻, calc. 423.0656.
  • Data: dye extracted directly from dyed fiber by pulsed laser, negative-ion mode HRMS: m/z = 423.0647 (100%), 424.0681 (22.5%), 425.0605 (4.21%). Given: ³²S 31.9721 (95.02%), ³⁴S 33.9679 (4.21%).
  • Asked: (a) explain origin and relative abundances of each peak; (b) the spectrum also shows m/z 425.0715 — explain its origin.
  • Answer [H]: 423.0647 = M⁻ (all ¹²C, ³²S). 424.0681 = M+1 mostly ¹³C (21 C × 1.1% ≈ 23% → 22.5%). 425.0605 = ³⁴S isotopologue (4.21% matches ³⁴S abundance; mass +1.9958). (b) 425.0715 = ion containing two ¹³C atoms (M+2 from ¹³C₂; +2×1.00335 = 425.0714), distinguishable from ³⁴S peak by HRMS.
  • Concept: isotope patterns, HRMS mass defects (¹³C vs ³⁴S).

15.70 — book p.708 — footnote 2

  • Citation: J. Am. Chem. Soc. 1952, 74, 4070–4073.
  • Compound: ethyl 2-oxocyclohexanecarboxylate (A, keto) ⇌ enol tautomer B (ethyl 2-hydroxycyclohex-1-enecarboxylate). A: CCOC(=O)C1CCCCC1=O; B: CCOC(=O)C1=C(O)CCCC1 [H].
  • Data: IR of equilibrium mixture shows 4 peaks in 1600–1850 cm⁻¹: 1620, 1660, 1720, 1740.
  • Asked: (a) assign the 4 peaks, justify position of 1660; (b) for most simple ketones enol concentration is negligible; here B is significant — why?
  • Answer [H]: 1740 = ester C=O of A; 1720 = ketone C=O of A; 1660 = ester C=O of B, lowered by conjugation with C=C and intramolecular H-bond with enol OH; 1620 = enol C=C (conjugated, polarized → strong). (b) Enol stabilized by conjugation (C=C–C=O) and intramolecular H-bond (6-membered chelate) of the β-keto ester.
  • Concept: keto–enol tautomerism, conjugation lowering C=O frequency.

15.71 — book p.708 — footnote 3

  • Citation: J. Chem. Ed. 2008, 85, 832–833.
  • Compounds: A = 2-(4-bromophenyl)ethanol OCCc1ccc(Br)cc1; B = 1-(4-bromophenyl)ethanol CC(O)c1ccc(Br)cc1 (both C8H9BrO, M 200/202).
  • Data: Sample X: m/z 202, 200, 187, 185, 159, 157, 121. Sample Y: m/z 202, 200, 171, 169, 121.
  • Asked: match A/B with X/Y, give structures for all peaks.
  • Answer [H]: X = B: 202/200 M⁺• (⁸¹Br/⁷⁹Br); 187/185 = M–CH3 (α-cleavage → ArCH=OH⁺); 159/157 = loss of CO from 187/185 (BrC6H4⁺… C6H6Br⁺); 121 = M–Br (C8H9O⁺). Y = A: 202/200 M⁺•; 171/169 = M–CH2OH (31) → 4-bromobenzyl (tropylium) cation; 121 = M–Br.
  • Concept: α-cleavage of alcohols, benzylic cleavage, Br isotope doublets.

15.72 — book p.709 — footnote 4

  • Citation: Anal. Chem. 1954, 26, 1428–1431.
  • Compound: myosmine (tobacco alkaloid related to nicotine). Originally proposed structure (drawn): 3-(4,5-dihydro-1H-pyrrol-2-yl)pyridine (enamine, N–H) C1CNC(=C1)c1cccnc1. Correct tautomer: 3-(3,4-dihydro-2H-pyrrol-5-yl)pyridine (1-pyrroline imine) C1CC(=NC1)c1cccnc1 [H].
  • Data: IR spectrum given (no N–H band 3300–3500; bands ~3000–2850; very strong 1621 cm⁻¹).
  • Asked: (a) why IR inconsistent with original structure; (b) myosmine exists mainly as a tautomer — draw it, explain fit; (c) source of very strong 1621 cm⁻¹.
  • Answer [H]: (a) no N–H stretch. (b) imine tautomer (C=N in ring, no N–H). (c) C=N stretch (conjugated with pyridine).

15.73 — book p.709 — footnote 5

  • Citation: J. Am. Chem. Soc. 1959, 81, 4671–4673.
  • Compounds: 1 = 6,7-dihydro-5H-pyrrolo[1,2-d]tetrazole (bicyclic tetrazole) C1CC2=NN=NN2C1; 2 = 5-azido-3,4-dihydro-2H-pyrrole (2-azido-1-pyrroline) [N-]=[N+]=NC1=NCCC1 [H].
  • Data: 1 ⇌ 2 rapidly; IR of 1 shows no signal in 2120–2160 cm⁻¹.
  • Asked: why does absence of this band show 1 is favored over 2?
  • Answer [H]: azide (–N3) asymmetric stretch is a strong band ~2100–2160 cm⁻¹; its absence means negligible azide tautomer (valence tautomerism azide–tetrazole lies on tetrazole side).

15.74 — book p.709 (도전 문제) — footnote 6

  • Citation: Appl. Spectrosc. 1960, 14, 95–97.
  • Compound: cyclohexanone O=C1CCCCC1.
  • Data: low-res base peak m/z 55; HRMS shows two peaks 55.0183 and 55.0546 (relative 86.7 : 13.3).
  • Asked: molecular formulas of ions for each.
  • Answer [H]: 55.0183 = C3H3O⁺ (calc. 55.018); 55.0546 = C4H7⁺ (calc. 55.054).

15.75 — book p.709 — footnote 7

  • Citation: J. Org. Chem. 1977, 42, 126–129.
  • Compound: pyrrolidine amide of a cyclopropane fatty acid (from study of cyclopropane derivatives of fatty acids) — drawn: long alkyl–cyclopropane–(CH2)n–C(=O)–N(pyrrolidinyl) (e.g., dihydrosterculic acid pyrrolidide; chain lengths approx.).
  • Data: base peak m/z 113.
  • Asked: reasonable structure for m/z 113 ion and mechanism.
  • Answer [H]: McLafferty rearrangement (γ-H transfer to C=O, β-cleavage) → enol radical cation CH2=C(OH)–N(C4H8)⁺• , C6H11NO⁺•, m/z 113.

15.76 — book p.709 — footnote 8

  • Citation: Anal. Chem. 2011, 83, 4738–4744.
  • Compound: pinolenic acid (C17H29CO2H), all-cis triene from pine nuts (appetite suppressant); = (5Z,9Z,12Z)-octadeca-5,9,12-trienoic acid OC(=O)CCC/C=C\CC/C=C\C/C=C\CCCCC [H, structure from knowledge; consistent with data].
  • Data: ESI with ozone present (ozonolysis during ionization); positive-ion mode peaks m/z 279, 211, 171, 117; without ozone only 279.
  • Asked: (a) structures for each peak and of pinolenic acid; (b) predict four main peaks in negative mode and assign.
  • Answer [H]: 279 = [M+H]⁺ (M = 278). Ozonolytic aldehydes (+H⁺): 211 = OHC–C11 chain retaining C1–C12 (C12H18O3+H, cleavage of Δ12); 171 = C1–C9 aldehyde acid (C9H14O3+H, Δ9); 117 = OHC(CH2)3COOH + H (C5H8O3+H, Δ5). Hence double bonds at C5, C9, C12. (b) Negative mode [M–H]⁻: 277, 209, 169, 115.

15.77 — book p.709 — footnote 9

  • Citation: J. Am. Chem. Soc. 1957, 79, 243–247.
  • Compounds: two steroid chlorohydrins (5α-steroid A-ring): 1 has OH on C2 and Cl on C3 (Cl hashed, drawn diaxial-type trans arrangement); 2 has Cl on C2 and OH (bold) on C3 — i.e., trans-2,3-chlorohydrins differing in conformational relationship (1 = trans-diaxial; 2 = trans-diequatorial) [M].
  • Data: dilute CS2 IR: 1 O–H at 3617 cm⁻¹; 2 at 3594 cm⁻¹.
  • Asked: (a) why 2 absorbs lower; (b) which signal is expected broader and why.
  • Answer [M]: In 2 OH and Cl are diequatorial (gauche, ~60°) → intramolecular O–H···Cl hydrogen bond weakens O–H → lower wavenumber; in 1 diaxial (anti, 180°), no intramolecular H-bond → free OH 3617. Dilution excludes intermolecular H-bonding. (b) Signal of 2 (H-bonded) broader.

15.78 — book p.710 — footnote 10

  • Citation: J. Am. Chem. Soc. 2006, 128, 58–59.
  • Compound: phosphatidic acid (anion) with sn-1 palmitoyl (C15H31CO) and sn-2 C17H33CO (one cis alkene) — drawn as HO–P(=O)(O⁻)–O–CH2–CH(OCOC17H33)–CH2–OCOC15H31.
  • Data: negative-mode MS [M]⁻ at m/z 673 (calc. C37H70O8P⁻ 673.48); with ozone new peak m/z 563. With MeOH solvent: 563 and 611; with EtOH: 563 stays, 611 → 625.
  • Asked: (a) full structure showing alkene position; (b) explain solvent results.
  • Answer [H]: loss of 110 (C9H18 replaced by O) → aldehyde from ozonolysis at Δ9: sn-2 = oleoyl, (9Z)-octadec-9-enoyl → PA(16:0/18:1 Δ9Z). SMILES: CCCCCCCCCCCCCCCC(=O)OCC(COP(=O)(O)[O-])OC(=O)CCCCCCC/C=C\CCCCCCCC. (b) Criegee carbonyl oxide (aldehyde + O, +16) trapped by solvent alcohol → α-alkoxy hydroperoxide: +16+32 (MeOH) = 611; +16+46 (EtOH) = 625; aldehyde 563 independent of solvent.

Chapter 16 — 핵자기공명 분광법 (Nuclear Magnetic Resonance Spectroscopy)

Summary pages: book p.753–755 (PDF p9–11). Problems p.756–761 (PDF p12–17). References: book p.761 (PDF p17).

Key concepts (summary 16.1–16.13)

  • NMR basics: spin, magnetic moment aligned with/against field, rf → spin flip (resonance); shielding/deshielding by diamagnetism; FT-NMR (pulse, FID); deuterated solvent + TMS.
  • Chemical equivalence: homotopic (rotation) / enantiotopic (reflection) = equivalent; diastereotopic = not; substitution (replacement) test.
  • Chemical shift δ: downfield/upfield; methyl 0.9, methylene 1.2, methine 1.7 ppm; corrections: +2.5 for O of alcohol/ether, +3.0 for O of ester, +1.0 for carbonyl; diamagnetic anisotropy deshields aromatic H.
  • Integration (step curve); multiplicity n+1 rule, coupling constant J (Hz); equivalent protons don't split each other; no coupling across >3 σ bonds; OH protons labile (no coupling); complex splitting → multiplets.
  • ¹H NMR analysis workflow: HDI → number of signals/integration → (δ, m, I) → fragments → assemble → verify.
  • ¹³C NMR: 1.1% natural abundance; broadband decoupling (singlets); off-resonance decoupling; δ 0–220 ppm (C=O 160–220, sp² 100–150, sp 65–90/C–N, C–O 50–100, sp³ 0–50); number of signals by symmetry.
  • DEPT: DEPT-90 shows only CH; DEPT-135 CH/CH3 positive, CH2 negative, quaternary C absent.

Literature-footnoted problems (refs 1–7)

Footnote 1 — not located in the supplied pages

  • Citation: Food Chem. 2016, 196, 211–219.

16.66 — book p.759 — footnote 2

  • Citation: Angew. Chem. Int. Ed. 2011, 50, 8387–8390.
  • Problem: deduce structure of C6H10O4 from IR, ¹H NMR, ¹³C NMR.
  • Data (read from spectra): IR strong 1747 cm⁻¹ (ester C=O), no O–H. ¹H: ~5.1 ppm (q, 1H), ~3.75 (s, 3H), ~2.1 (s, 3H), ~1.5 (d, 3H). ¹³C: ~171, ~170 (two C=O near 170), ~69, ~52, ~21, ~17 (solvent CDCl3 at 77).
  • HDI = 2 (two C=O).
  • Answer [H]: methyl 2-acetoxypropanoate (methyl O-acetyllactate), CC(=O)OC(C)C(=O)OC.
  • Concept: combined IR/¹H/¹³C structure elucidation; deshielded CH (O-acyl, α to ester) ~5.1 ppm.

16.72 — book p.761 (도전 문제) — footnote 3

  • Citation: Org. Lett. 2012, 14, 4770–4773.
  • Compound: brevianamide S (antitubercular natural product, Chinese Bohai Sea marine sediment-derived fungus) — dimeric diketopiperazine alkaloid: two (Z)-3-ylidene-diketopiperazines bearing 2-(1,1-dimethylallyl = reverse prenyl)indol-3-yl groups; the two dehydroproline-fused units are linked C–C through their pyrroline carbons (as drawn).
  • Given shifts: indole N–H 7.7 ppm; diketopiperazine amide N–H 7.4 ppm; N–CH2 (pyrroline, highlighted) 4.1 ppm.
  • Asked: predict chemical shifts and multiplicity of every signal in the ¹H NMR.
  • Expected answer [M]: reverse-prenyl CH3 singlets ~1.5 (6H, s); vinyl CH= ~6.1 (dd), =CH2 ~5.1–5.2 (2 × dd); aromatic indole H 7.0–7.5 (d/t); exocyclic vinyl CH= (ylidene) ~7.0 (s); N–CH2 4.1 (t); ring CH2 ~3.0 (t); NH 7.4 & 7.7 (s, broad). Concept: prediction of δ/multiplicity in complex molecules.

16.73 — book p.761 — footnote 4

  • Citation: J. Am. Chem. Soc. 1973, 95, 3437–3438.
  • Reaction: 1,3,6-cyclononatriene (1, R = H; C1=CC=CCC=CCC1 – ring numbering C1=C2, C3=C4, C6=C7, sp³ C5, C8, C9) or its 8,8-dimethyl derivative (2, R = CH3) + KNH2 / liq. NH3 → anion 1a / 2a (deprotonation at the doubly allylic C5 → cyclic heptatrienyl anion spanning 7 carbons, C8/C9 sp³ remain).
  • Asked: (a) draw four resonance structures of 1a; (b) number of ¹H signals for 1a and 2a; (c) 1a: signals 3.74 and 3.39 ppm (H on C1 and C3), H on C2 and C4 at 5.63 and 5.52 ppm — explain the two separated groups; (d) for 2a: how many signals in 3–4 ppm and in 5–6 ppm?
  • Answer [M]: (a) negative charge on C1, C3, C5, C7 of the heptatrienyl (anion numbered C1–C7 symmetrically, C4 central). (b) 1a (mirror symmetry): H1/H7, H2/H6, H3/H5, H4, C8/C9-H2 → 5 signals (ignoring possible diastereotopicity); 2a (gem-dimethyl at C8 only, no symmetry): 7 vinylic H all distinct + CH2 + 2 CH3 (diastereotopic) → ~10. (c) charge-bearing carbons (odd positions) are shielded (3.4–3.7 ppm), nodal positions (C2, C4, C6) are normal vinylic (5.5–5.6). (d) 2a: 4 signals in 3–4 ppm (H1, H3, H5, H7) and 3 signals in 5–6 ppm (H2, H4, H6).

16.74 — book p.761 — footnote 5

  • Citation: J. Chem. Ed. 2003, 80, 1439–1443.
  • Reaction: 4-nitro-3-(trifluoromethyl)aniline (1) Nc1ccc([N+](=O)[O-])c(C(F)(F)F)c1 + isobutyryl chloride (2-methylpropanoyl chloride), pyridine → flutamide (2) CC(C)C(=O)Nc1ccc([N+](=O)[O-])c(C(F)(F)F)c1 (prostate-cancer drug) [H].
  • Protons: Ha = ortho to NO2 (C5), Hb = ortho to NH2 (C6), Hc = between CF3 and NH2 (C2).
  • Asked: (a) which aromatic H of 1 is most downfield? justify by resonance & inductive effects; (b) do these H shift upfield or downfield on going 1 → 2, and which is affected most?
  • Answer [M]: (a) Ha (ortho to strongly withdrawing NO2, and not ortho/para to the donating NH2; Hb and Hc are ortho to NH2 → shielded by resonance donation). (b) All shift downfield because the amide N lone pair is delocalized into C=O (weaker donor); largest change for the protons ortho to N (Hb and Hc; Hb typically largest, also deshielded by C=O anisotropy) — Ha (meta to N) least affected.

16.75 — book p.761 — footnote 6

  • Citation: J. Chem. Ed. 2006, 83, 287–289.
  • Compounds: coumarin (1) O=c1ccc2ccccc2o1; 2 = 3-acetyl-7-(diethylamino)coumarin CCN(CC)c1ccc2cc(C(C)=O)c(=O)oc2c1.
  • Data: two lowest-field ¹H signals of 2 at 7.38 and 8.42 ppm.
  • Asked: (a) identify the two protons, justify using resonance; (b) is IR C3=C4 π-bond stretch stronger in 1 or 2?
  • Answer [M–H]: (a) 8.42 = H4 (β-H of an α,β-unsaturated dicarbonyl — δ+ at C4 by resonance with both lactone C=O and acetyl C=O); 7.38 = H5 (ortho to C4, peri; the only aromatic H not ortho/para to the NEt2 donor — H6 and H8 are shielded). (b) Stronger in 2: push–pull polarization (Et2N donor → carbonyl acceptors) gives a much larger dipole change for the C3=C4 vibration.

16.76 — book p.761 — footnote 7

  • Citation: Tetrahedron Lett. 2011, 52, 4182–4185.
  • Reaction: 2,6-bis((trimethylsilyl)ethynyl)benzaldehyde (1) O=Cc1c(C#C[Si](C)(C)C)cccc1C#C[Si](C)(C)C + Fischer carbene 2 = (CO)5Cr=C(OMe)CH2CH2CH=CH2 (pentacarbonyl[1-methoxypent-4-enylidene]chromium) → hydronaphthacene (five fused six-membered rings) bis(cyclohexenone) with two TMS groups and a tertiary OH, formed as diastereomers 3 (R) and 4 (S) differing at one stereocenter (highlighted ring-fusion CH).
  • Asked: how to distinguish 3 and 4 using only ¹³C NMR.
  • Answer [M]: the pentacycle is pseudo-symmetric about the plane through C–OH and the central arene; in one diastereomer the two ring-fusion H's have the relative configuration making the halves mirror-related (meso-like, Cs) → about half the number of ¹³C signals; in the other diastereomer the halves are inequivalent → every carbon gives its own signal (roughly twice as many). Count signals.

Chapter 17 — 콘쥬게이션 파이계와 페리고리 협동반응 (Conjugated Pi Systems and Pericyclic Reactions)

Summary pages: book p.802–806 (PDF p18–22: reaction review p.802–803, concept review p.803–804, skill review p.805–806). Problems p.806–810 (PDF p22–26). References: book p.810 (PDF p26).

Key reactions / concepts

  • Diene preparation: allylic halide or vicinal dihalide + t-BuOK (elimination).
  • Electrophilic addition to 1,3-butadiene: HBr → 1,2-adduct (kinetic; 71% at 0 °C) vs 1,4-adduct (thermodynamic; 85% at 40 °C); Br2 → 1,2- and 1,4-dibromides; allylic carbocation resonance.
  • Diels–Alder [4+2]: cyclohexene products; retro-DA at high T; stereospecific (cis-dienophile → cis, trans → trans, racemic); alkyne dienophiles (DMAD → 1,4-cyclohexadiene); cyclopentadiene gives bicyclic, endo preferred; s-cis diene required; EWG on dienophile accelerates; HOMO(diene)→LUMO(dienophile).
  • MO theory: HOMO/LUMO, frontier orbitals, photo-excited state; orbital symmetry conservation: thermal [4+2] allowed, thermal [2+2] forbidden, photochemical [2+2] allowed.
  • Electrocyclic reactions: hexatriene ⇌ cyclohexadiene; butadiene ⇌ cyclobutene; 4π thermal conrotatory/photochemical disrotatory; 6π thermal disrotatory/photochemical conrotatory.
  • Sigmatropic: [3,3] Cope (15:85 equilibrium example) and Claisen (allyl vinyl ether → γ,δ-unsaturated carbonyl, 10:90).
  • UV–Vis: π→π*, λmax, molar absorptivity ε, Beer's law; more conjugation → longer λmax; chromophore/auxochrome; Woodward–Fieser rules (base 217, +30 per extra double bond, +5 per alkyl, +5 exocyclic, +39 homoannular diene); color if λmax 400–700 nm; vision: rhodopsin, 11-cis-retinal → all-trans photoisomerization (Nakanishi locked analog).

Literature-footnoted problems (refs 1–10)

17.71 — book p.809 — footnote 1

  • Citation: J. Am. Chem. Soc. 2011, 133, 17634–17637.
  • Context: total synthesis of the toxin gelsemoxonine (Gelsemium elegans, SE Asia).
  • Substrate (as drawn): a cis-divinylcyclopropane — cyclopropane bearing (i) a trimethylsilyl enol ether (Me3SiO–C=) whose alkene is part of a small O-containing ring (oxetane/ether bridge drawn above), (ii) an exocyclic (E)-alkylidene at C3 of an N-methoxy-oxindole (N–OMe, 2-oxindole), and (iii) a CH2OR (R = protecting group) on the quaternary cyclopropane carbon. Heated at 70 °C.
  • Asked: predict product and mechanism.
  • Answer [M]: divinylcyclopropane → cycloheptadiene rearrangement ([3,3] sigmatropic, boat TS, cis-divinyl): the cyclopropane C–C σ bond between the two vinyl-bearing carbons breaks, new σ bond forms between the vinyl termini (enol-ether carbon terminus and oxindole C3) → a spiro-oxindole fused 1,4-cycloheptadiene bearing the silyl enol ether (azepane-free; spiro at oxindole C3), setting the gelsemoxonine core with relative stereochemistry from the boat TS.

17.72 — book p.809 — footnote 2

  • Citation: J. Photochem. Photobiol. A 2006, 184, 177–183.
  • Compound 1: 3,4-bis(2,4,5-trimethylthiophen-3-yl)furan-2,5-dione (dithienylethene photochromic maleic anhydride) O=C1OC(=O)C(c2c(C)sc(C)c2C)=C1c1c(C)sc(C)c1C [H].
  • Reaction: UV light → 6π electrocyclic ring closure → 2; visible light → ring-opening back to 1 (photochromism).
  • Asked: draw 2, chiral or not, mechanism.
  • Answer [H]: photochemical 6π → conrotatory; new C–C bond between the two thiophene C2 carbons, each now sp³ bearing CH3, the two methyls trans (anti). 2 = CC1=C(C)SC2(C)C1=C1C(=O)OC(=O)C1=C1C(C)=C(C)SC12C (closed, colored ring isomer, C18H18O3S2). 2 is chiral (two stereocenters, C2-symmetric trans, no mirror plane) and forms as a racemate (conrotation either way).

17.73 — book p.809 — footnote 3

  • Citation: J. Am. Chem. Soc. 1971, 93, 3836–3837.
  • Context: first total synthesis of chelidonine (cytotoxic, Chelidonium majus roots).
  • Substrate: benzocyclobutene (methylenedioxy-substituted) bearing N(R)–CH2–Ar on the cyclobutene sp³ carbon; Ar = methylenedioxyphenyl with an ortho ethynyl group. Heat 120 °C.
  • Product (drawn): tetracyclic B/C-hexahydrobenzo[c]phenanthridine skeleton (N–R, two methylenedioxy groups, ring C dihydro with C=C).
  • Asked: mechanism.
  • Answer [H]: (1) thermal 4π conrotatory electrocyclic ring opening of benzocyclobutene → o-quinodimethane (o-xylylene); (2) intramolecular Diels–Alder of the o-quinodimethane (diene) with the alkyne (dienophile) → ring re-aromatizes; gives the 1,4-cyclohexadiene ring of product (trans/cis-fusion from conrotation sense).

17.74 — book p.809 — footnote 4

  • Citation: Org. Lett. 2010, 12, 1152–1155.
  • Context: synthesis studies toward atropurpuran (diterpene).
  • Substrate: acyclic aminotetraene R2N–CH=CH–CH=CH–CH=CH–CH2–N(Ts)–CH2CH=CH2 (triene conjugated with amino donor; N-allyl sulfonamide tether). Heat 135 °C.
  • Product: tricyclic — bicyclo[2.2.2]octene fused to a N-Ts piperidine, with R2N on the bicyclooctene alkene.
  • Answer [H]: (1) thermal 6π disrotatory electrocyclization of the (Z-central) triene → 1,3-cyclohexadiene (amino-substituted); (2) intramolecular Diels–Alder between the cyclohexadiene (diene) and the tethered allyl alkene (dienophile) → bicyclo[2.2.2]octene + piperidine ring (tricyclic).

17.75 — book p.809 (도전 문제) — footnote 5

  • Citation: Org. Lett. 2001, 3, 723–726.
  • Reaction: 2 equiv. 4-(vinyloxy)butyl (E)-2-oxo-4-phenylbut-3-enoate O=C(C(=O)OCCCCOC=C)/C=C/c1ccccc1 heated at 195 °C → macrocyclic dimer containing two 3,4-dihydro-2H-pyran rings (each: 6-carboxylate on the C5=C6 enol-ether double bond, 4-Ph, 2-alkoxy acetal), joined head-to-tail by two –O(CH2)4O– / ester linkers.
  • Asked: mechanism.
  • Answer [H]: two sequential intermolecular hetero-Diels–Alder (inverse-electron-demand oxa-DA) reactions: the β,γ-unsaturated α-ketoester O=C–C=C acts as 1-oxadiene; vinyl ether of a second molecule = electron-rich dienophile → 2-alkoxy-3,4-dihydro-2H-pyran; the first DA gives a dimer which then cyclizes by an intramolecular second hetero-DA → macrocycle (head-to-tail).

17.76 — book p.809 — footnote 6

  • Citation: Org. Lett. 2012, 14, 502–505.
  • Reaction: 2,2':5',2''-terfuran (trifuran) + maleimide ⇌ only adduct at a terminal furan (7-oxabicyclo[2.2.1]heptene with bifuranyl at bridgehead); adduct at the central furan (two furyl groups on both bridgeheads) not observed.
  • Asked: rational explanation.
  • Answer [M]: furan DA is reversible (thermodynamic control). Reaction at the terminal furan retains a conjugated bifuran π system; reaction at the central ring would destroy conjugation across the whole oligomer (leaving two isolated furans) and place substituents at both bridgeheads (steric), so it is thermodynamically disfavored. (Also: the terminal ring's diene has an unsubstituted, more accessible terminus.)

17.77 — book p.810 — footnote 7

  • Citation: J. Am. Chem. Soc. 2012, 134, 9199–9208.
  • Reaction: tricyclic cyclooctyne A (dibenzo-azacyclooctynone, N-methyl lactam; CN1C(=O)c2ccccc2C#Cc2ccccc21) + benzyl azide → triazoles B + C (strain-promoted azide–alkyne [3+2] cycloaddition, copper-free click).
  • Asked: (a) mechanism for B; (b) C is a constitutional (regio)isomer formed in ~equal amount — draw it; (c) why alkynes D (2-butyne, no reaction), E (cyclooctyne with OCH2-C6H4-COOH ether, 375× slower than A), F (A with two aryl methyls, 1000× slower) are slower.
  • Answer [H/M]: (a) concerted 1,3-dipolar cycloaddition (4π dipole + 2π), driven by relief of ring strain in the bent cyclooctyne. (b) C = regioisomeric triazole with N-benzyl on the other triazole carbon side (adjacent to the other aryl ring, i.e., the benzyl N next to the lactam-N-bearing arene vs. carbonyl-bearing arene). (c) D: linear unstrained alkyne — no strain release, high barrier. E: less strained (no fused benzo rings / fewer sp² centers raising strain) and lacks the electronic activation of A. F: the methyl ortho to the alkyne sterically hinders azide approach (and alters ring strain/electronics).

17.78 — book p.810 — footnote 8

  • Citation: Aust. J. Chem. 1982, 35, 2247–2256.
  • Context: endiandric acids (Endiandra introrsa, Australia) are isolated as racemates; proposed biosynthesis (Black cascade) from achiral polyene 1: an acyclic (E,Z,Z,E)-conjugated tetraene with chains CH2CH=CH–CH2CO2R and CH2CH=CH–CH=CH–Ph.
  • Asked: (a) first step = thermal electrocyclic reaction forming 8-membered ring 2; draw 2, explain racemic; (b) 2 undergoes another electrocyclization to the endiandric acid G skeleton — mechanism; (c) endiandric acid C forms from G by intramolecular DA — draw C and mechanism.
  • Answer: (a) [H] 8π thermal conrotatory electrocyclization → cycloocta-1,3,5-triene 2 with two trans side chains at the new sp³ carbons; achiral substrate, no chiral catalyst → both conrotatory senses equally likely → racemate. (b) [H] 6π thermal disrotatory electrocyclization of the cyclooctatriene → cis-bicyclo[4.2.0]octa-2,4-diene (endiandric acid G framework). (c) [L–M] intramolecular [4+2]: cyclohexadiene of the bicyclo[4.2.0]octadiene = diene, a side-chain C=C (tethered on the cyclobutane) = dienophile → tetracyclic endiandric acid C (exact dienophile choice not verified from page).

17.79 — book p.810 — footnote 9

  • Citation: J. Am. Chem. Soc. 2008, 130, 6281–6287.
  • Compounds: roquefortine C (Penicillium roqueforti; blue cheese) and isoroquefortine C (its diastereomer = geometric isomer about the dehydrohistidine exocyclic C=C; drawn with the imidazole rotated so the ring N= faces the diketopiperazine N–H). Isoroquefortine C is thermodynamically more stable yet not found in nature.
  • Asked: explain stability difference.
  • Answer [M]: in isoroquefortine C the diketopiperazine amide N–H forms an intramolecular hydrogen bond to the basic imidazole sp² nitrogen (planar conjugated 6/7-membered H-bonded array), and reduced A(1,3)-type strain between the imidazole and the C=O; roquefortine C lacks this H-bond. Nature's (enzymatic, kinetically controlled) biosynthesis gives roquefortine C, and isomerization does not occur under physiological conditions.

17.80 — book p.810 — footnote 10

  • Citation: J. Am. Chem. Soc. 2000, 122, 190–191.
  • Reaction: thioamide 1 = 1-propanethioyl-(2R,5R)-trans-2,5-diphenylpyrrolidine (C2-symmetric) CCC(=S)N1[C@@H](c2ccccc2)CC[C@H]1c1ccccc1 (stereodescriptors as drawn, [M]); 1) BuLi; 2) 2-cyclohexylideneethyl bromide → ketene N,S-acetal 2 → thio-Claisen [3,3] → 3: thioamide with α-CH(CH3) attached to a quaternary cyclohexyl carbon bearing vinyl (2-methyl-3-(1-vinylcyclohexyl)propanethioyl… i.e. α-stereocenter created).
  • Asked: (a) structure of 2 with Z C=C (use retro of 3); (b) mechanism 1 → 2; (c) explain stereochemical outcome at new stereocenter.
  • Answer [H/M]: (a) 2 = (Z)-S-(2-cyclohexylideneethyl) ketene N,S-acetal: CH3CH=C(N-pyrrolidinyl)(S–CH2CH=C(CH2)5), C/C=C(/SCC=C1CCCCC1)N1[C@@H](c2ccccc2)CC[C@H]1c1ccccc1. (b) BuLi deprotonates α-CH2 → (Z)-thioenolate (A1,3 strain with the bulky pyrrolidine); S-alkylation (SN2) by the allylic bromide → 2. (c) [3,3] via chair TS; the C2-symmetric 2,5-diphenylpyrrolidine shields one face of the ketene N,S-acetal, so the allyl group is delivered to one face → single configuration at the new α-stereocenter (Z geometry + chair → defined relative/absolute configuration).

Chapter 18 — 방향족 화합물 (Aromatic Compounds)

Summary pages: book p.842–844 (PDF p27–29: reaction review + concept review p.842–843, skill review p.843–844). Problems p.844–849 (PDF p29–34). References: book p.849 (PDF p34).

Key reactions / concepts

  • Benzylic reactions: oxidation (Na2Cr2O7/H2SO4 or KMnO4, heat → benzoic acid, needs benzylic H); NBS/heat radical benzylic bromination; benzylic halides undergo SN1, SN2, E1, E2; dehydration of benzylic alcohols.
  • Reduction: catalytic hydrogenation of benzene (Ni, 100 atm, 150 °C) → cyclohexane; vinyl group selectively hydrogenated; Birch reduction (Na, CH3OH, NH3) → 1,4-cyclohexadiene — EDG (alkyl) carbons not reduced, EWG (C=O) carbons reduced.
  • Nomenclature: monosubstituted (common names toluene, phenol, aniline, anisole…), ortho/meta/para, locants, alphabetical order.
  • Benzene structure (bond order 1.5), resonance; stability (heats of hydrogenation, stabilization energy); MO picture; Hückel 4n+2 rule; Frost circle; antiaromatic cyclobutadiene, tub-shaped nonaromatic COT; annulenes ([10]annulene nonaromatic due to steric strain); cyclopentadienyl anion & tropylium cation aromatic; heterocycles: pyridine lone pair localized (sp²), pyrrole lone pair delocalized (part of 6π); lone-pair participation analysis (histamine example); PAHs.
  • Spectroscopy: aromatic IR bands; ¹H benzylic 2–3 ppm, aryl H ~7 ppm; ¹³C 100–150 ppm.

Literature-footnoted problems (refs 1–10)

18.66 — book p.848 — footnote 1

  • Citation: Tetrahedron Lett. 2012, 53, 5385–5388.
  • Compound A: benzo-fused 14-membered dehydroannulene-type macrocycle with quinoidal π system: ring contains o-phenylene, four C≡C, one cis CH=CH and two ring sp² carbons each bearing exocyclic =C(CN)2 (dicyanomethylene, quinoidal). 2e⁻ reduction → B: dianion; drawn with negative charge on each C(CN)2 and an unpaired electron (dot) on each ring carbon.
  • Asked: (a) is the 14-membered ring of B aromatic? (b) resonance structures showing delocalization of negative charge; (c) at least three resonance structures showing delocalization of unpaired (odd) electrons.
  • Answer [M]: (a) Yes — pairing the two ring "radicals" across the ring gives a closed-shell 14-π-electron perimeter (benzo C=C 2e + 4 alkynes' out-of-plane π 8e + CH=CH 2e + two ring carbons 2e = 14 = 4n+2, n = 3) → aromatic dehydro[14]annulene; aromatization is the driving force for facile reduction of the quinoidal A. (b) C(CN)2⁻ charge delocalized onto both nitrile N atoms (ketenimine-type resonance, ⁻N=C=C). (c) unpaired electrons move along the conjugated perimeter: onto alkyne carbons (allenyl-type forms), across CH=CH, into the benzo ring.

18.67 — book p.848 (도전 문제) — footnote 2

  • Citation: J. Am. Chem. Soc. 1959, 81, 4671–4673. (same reference as ch.15 fn 5)
  • Compounds: 1 = tetrazolo[5,1-?]-1,2-benzisothiazole (fused tetrazole, S–N bridgehead) c1ccc2c(c1)sn1nnnc12; 2 = 3-azido-1,2-benzisothiazole [N-]=[N+]=Nc1nsc2ccccc12; rapid equilibrium 1 ⇌ 2.
  • Asked: in 2 only one of the four N remains in a ring; does that N's hybridization change on isomerization? explain.
  • Answer [M–H]: That N (bonded to S) is sp² in both. In 1 it is a 3-connected (pyrrole-like) N whose lone pair occupies a p orbital contributing to the aromatic sextet of the tetrazole; in 2 it is a 2-connected (pyridine-like) N with its lone pair in an sp² orbital (isothiazole ring aromaticity uses one p electron). Hybridization unchanged; lone-pair orbital location changes.

18.68 — book p.848 — footnote 3

  • Citation: J. Am. Chem. Soc. 2005, 127, 13070–13078.
  • Context: Titan atmosphere chemistry modelling: 1,2,5,6-tetracyanocyclooctatetraene N#CC1=C(C#N)C=CC(C#N)=C(C#N)C=C1 formed by gas-phase Mg⁺•-mediated reactions; all four nitrile N lone pairs bind one Mg⁺•.
  • Asked: (a) draw the complex (dashed lines for N…Mg); (b) why 1,2,4,5-tetracyanobenzene cannot form a similar complex.
  • Answer [H]: (a) COT is non-planar tub-shaped; the four CN groups at 1,2,5,6 point to the same face (convergent), so Mg⁺ sits over the tub with four N→Mg dative bonds. (b) Tetracyanobenzene is planar (aromatic); its nitriles are linear and diverge in the ring plane, so the four N lone pairs cannot converge on one metal ion.

18.69(c) — book p.848 — footnote 4

  • Citation: J. Am. Chem. Soc. 1985, 107, 6678–6683.
  • Context: (a) naphthalene resonance → C1–C2 (1.36 Å) shorter than C2–C3 (1.42 Å) (C1–C2 double in 2 of 3 structures); (b) phenanthrene's 5 resonance structures → C9–C10 double in 4 of 5 → shortest ("bond fixation"); (c) phenanthrene + Br2 → C14H10Br2 — draw product.
  • Answer [H]: trans-9,10-dibromo-9,10-dihydrophenanthrene BrC1c2ccccc2-c2ccccc2C1Br — addition across the alkene-like C9=C10 bond (retains two benzene sextets).

18.70 — book p.848 — footnote 5

  • Citation: Angew. Chem. Int. Ed. 1988, 27, 1437–1455.
  • Topic: stable cyclobutadienes (normally dimerize by Diels–Alder).
  • Asked: (a) why tri-tert-butylcyclobutadiene (R1=R2=R3=t-Bu, R4=H) is stable; (b) why the push–pull derivative R1=R3=NEt2, R2=R4=CO2Et is stable; (c) why the same donor/acceptor pattern destabilizes 4n+2 systems.
  • Answer [M–H]: (a) kinetic (steric) stabilization: bulky t-Bu groups block approach for dimerization (DA). (b) captodative push–pull: zwitterionic resonance forms (iminium/enolate) delocalize charge and give the ring 2π-cation/allyl anion character, reducing cyclic 4π antiaromatic delocalization (thermodynamic stabilization) + sterics. (c) In aromatic 4n+2 rings, the polarization/charge localization disrupts the stabilizing cyclic delocalization (loss of aromaticity), so it is destabilizing; in antiaromatic rings breaking up cyclic delocalization is stabilizing.

18.71 — book p.848 — footnote 6

  • Citation: J. Org. Chem. 2004, 69, 549–554.
  • Reaction: cyclopentadiene-fused [14]annulene = 2,7-di-tert-butyl-trans-10b,10c-dimethyl-10b,10c-dihydropyrene with a fused cyclopentadiene ring; + KH → blue, stable cyclopentadienide anion. Internal CH3 ¹H shift moves from −3.9 to −1.8 ppm.
  • Asked: explain.
  • Answer [H]: the internal methyls sit inside the diatropic [14]annulene ring current (strongly shielded, −3.9). On deprotonation, the five-membered ring becomes an aromatic 6π cyclopentadienide; to share π electrons with this new aromatic ring, bond alternation in the macrocycle increases (benzannelation-type competition), weakening the [14]annulene ring current → methyls less shielded (−1.8 ppm).

18.72 — book p.848 — footnote 7

  • Citation: J. Nat. Prod. 2011, 74, 1972–1979.
  • Compound: antimalarial (vs. drug-resistant Plasmodium) bromo-β-carboline from a New Zealand ascidian (sea squirt): 7-bromo-1-(4-guanidinobutyl)-9H-pyrido[3,4-b]indole NC(=N)NCCCCc1nccc2c1[nH]c1cc(Br)ccc12 [H as drawn].
  • Asked: rank basicity of each N; product with 2 equiv HCl.
  • Answer [H]: guanidine (most basic; resonance-stabilized guanidinium) > pyridine-type N of β-carboline (sp² lone pair) > indole N–H (lone pair in aromatic sextet, non-basic). 2 HCl → dihydrochloride: guanidinium chloride + pyridinium (N2-H⁺) chloride.

18.73 — book p.849 — footnote 8

  • Citation: J. Org. Chem. 1964, 29, 2065–2066.
  • Data: m/z 39 peak in 17 of 20 N-heteroaromatics (5–84% of base peak), including 2-methylpyrazine, 2-aminopyrimidine, pyrazole, pyrrole; absent in imidazole, 3-amino-1,2,4-triazole, 4-amino-1,2,4-triazole.
  • Asked: structure of m/z 39 cation and consistency with data.
  • Answer [H]: C3H3⁺ cyclopropenyl (cyclopropenium) cation — aromatic (2π, Hückel n = 0). Requires three contiguous carbons in the parent; imidazole (C4–C5 only adjacent pair) and the triazoles lack a C–C–C unit, so no m/z 39.

18.74 — book p.849 — footnote 9

  • Citation: J. Org. Chem. 2002, 67, 7029–7036.
  • Reaction: 9,9'-bifluorenylidene (1, highly conjugated, planar) → −2e⁻ → dication 2 (two 9-fluorenyl cations joined by C9–C9', drawn twisted/perpendicular).
  • Asked: draw ≥4 resonance structures of 2; explain why the one with adjacent like charges (both + at C9/C9') is the most important contributor.
  • Answer [M]: other structures place + on ring carbons (ortho/para positions of the benzo rings), each of which disrupts a benzene aromatic sextet; the C9/C9' structure keeps all four benzene rings aromatic, and the two fluorenyl units are orthogonal (twisted), minimizing charge–charge/ π interaction. (Note: the fluorenyl cation five-membered ring is formally 4π antiaromatic; delocalization into it is disfavored.)

18.75 — book p.849 — footnote 10

  • Citation: Bioorg. Med. Chem. 2009, 17, 4047–4063.
  • Reaction: 5-ethyl-2-phenyl-6-(trifluoromethyl)pyrimidin-4(3H)-one CCc1c(C(F)(F)F)nc(-c2ccccc2)[nH]c1=O + NaOMe, propargyl bromide (HC≡CCH2Br) → A (herbicide; inhibits chlorophyll/β-carotene accumulation) = N3-propargyl pyrimidinone CCc1c(C(F)(F)F)nc(-c2ccccc2)n(CC#C)c1=O + B (inactive constitutional isomer, also acetylenic); A:B = 1:40.
  • Asked: (a) mechanism for A; (b) structure and mechanism of B; (c) why B favored.
  • Answer [H]: (a) NaOMe deprotonates N–H → ambident amidate (charge on N and O); N attacks propargyl bromide (SN2). (b) B = O-alkylation product, 5-ethyl-2-phenyl-4-(prop-2-yn-1-yloxy)-6-(trifluoromethyl)pyrimidine CCc1c(OCC#C)nc(-c2ccccc2)nc1C(F)(F)F, SN2 by O⁻. (c) O-alkylation gives a fully aromatic pyrimidine (aromatic stabilization), and steric hindrance at N3 (flanked by C=O and 2-phenyl) disfavors N-alkylation (CF3 also makes ring electron-poor, favoring charge on O).

Chapter 19 — 방향족 치환반응 (Aromatic Substitution Reactions)

Summary pages: book p.893–897 (PDF p35–39: reaction review p.893–894, concept review p.894–895, skill review p.895–897). Problems p.897–903 (PDF p39–45). References: book p.903 (PDF p45).

Key reactions / concepts

  • EAS of benzene: Br2/AlBr3 (or FeBr3) bromination; Cl2/AlCl3 chlorination; HNO3/H2SO4 nitration (NO2⁺); fuming H2SO4 sulfonation (SO3; reversible — dilute H2SO4 desulfonation); Friedel–Crafts alkylation (CH3Cl/AlCl3; carbocation rearrangements, polyalkylation, halide C must be sp³); Friedel–Crafts acylation (RCOCl/AlCl3; acylium ion, no rearrangement, no polyacylation) + Clemmensen reduction Zn(Hg)/HCl → alkyl; nitro → amine (Fe or Zn/HCl then NaOH); benzylic NBS (excess → CBr3); KMnO4 side-chain oxidation → benzoic acid.
  • Mechanism: sigma complex (arenium ion) formation (rate-determining, endergonic) then deprotonation restoring aromaticity.
  • Directing effects: activators (o/p) — strong (–NH2, –OH, –OR [OR moderate exception]), moderate (–OC(O)R, –NHC(O)R), weak (alkyl); deactivators — weak halogens (o/p directors), moderate (C=O, CN; meta), strong (–NO2, –NR3⁺, –CX3; meta). Multiple substituents: strongest activator dominates; steric effects; blocking groups (SO3H); retrosynthesis for polysubstituted benzenes; limitations (no nitration of anilines, no FC on moderately/strongly deactivated rings).
  • Nucleophilic aromatic substitution (SNAr): needs strong EWG (NO2), leaving group, EWG ortho/para to LG; addition–elimination via Meisenheimer complex (e.g., p-bromonitrobenzene + NaOH 70 °C → p-nitrophenol).
  • Elimination–addition (benzyne): chlorobenzene + NaOH 350 °C → phenol; p-chlorotoluene + NaNH2/NH3 → p- + m-toluidine; isotope labeling evidence.
  • Decision tree: electrophile → EAS; nucleophile + 3 SNAr conditions → SNAr; otherwise elimination–addition.

Literature-footnoted problems (refs 1–10)

19.83 — book p.901 — footnote 1

  • Citation: J. Org. Chem. 1973, 38, 493–499.
  • Reaction: 4-X-4'-Y-stilbenes + Br2 → 1,2-dibromo-1,2-diarylethanes. X = OMe fixed; rate constants k (×10³ L/mol·min): Y = OMe 220; Me 114; Cl 45; NO2 5.2.
  • Asked: rational explanation of trend, given that the stilbene C=C acts as nucleophile.
  • Answer [H]: rate-determining electrophilic attack of Br2 on C=C forms a bromonium/benzylic-carbocation-like intermediate with positive charge developing at the benzylic carbons; electron-donating Y (OMe > Me) stabilizes the cationic TS by resonance/hyperconjugation and increases alkene nucleophilicity; electron-withdrawing Y (Cl inductive, NO2 strongly by resonance+induction) destabilizes it → slower (Hammett ρ < 0).

19.84 — book p.901 — footnote 2

  • Citation: J. Org. Chem. 2012, 77, 5503–5514.
  • Context: route to aminotetralins (antidepressant candidates).
  • Reaction: 2-(2-bromo-5-methylphenyl)acetyl chloride O=C(Cl)Cc1cc(C)ccc1Br + H2C=CH2, AlCl3 → 8-bromo-5-methyl-3,4-dihydronaphthalen-2(1H)-one (8-bromo-5-methyl-2-tetralone) O=C1CCc2c(C)ccc(Br)c2C1 [H].
  • Answer [H]: AlCl3 + acid chloride → acylium ion; ethylene attacks acylium (aliphatic Friedel–Crafts acylation) → primary carbocation ArCH2C(=O)CH2CH2⁺ (or its chloride-trapped β-chloroketone, re-ionized by AlCl3) → intramolecular Friedel–Crafts alkylation at the ring carbon ortho to the CH2 (and ortho to CH3, activated) via arenium ion → deprotonation restores aromaticity → 2-tetralone.

19.85 — book p.901 — footnote 3

  • Citation: Org. Lett. 2007, 9, 2741–2743.
  • Reaction: 1-chloro-3-fluoro-2-nitrobenzene O=[N+]([O-])c1c(F)cccc1Cl + sodium (4-methoxyphenyl)acetylide → 1-chloro-3-[(4-methoxyphenyl)ethynyl]-2-nitrobenzene COc1ccc(C#Cc2cccc(Cl)c2[N+](=O)[O-])cc1 (F replaced, not Cl).
  • Asked: (a) mechanism; (b) identify RDS from regiochemistry.
  • Answer [H]: SNAr addition–elimination: acetylide adds to C–F carbon (ortho to NO2) → Meisenheimer complex (charge delocalized onto NO2) → loss of F⁻. (b) Nucleophilic addition (Meisenheimer complex formation) is rate-determining: highly electronegative F makes the ipso carbon more electrophilic and stabilizes the anionic TS; C–X bond breaking (where Cl⁻ would be the better LG) is not in the RDS, hence F > Cl.

19.86 — book p.901–902 — footnote 4

  • Citation: J. Am. Chem. Soc. 1967, 89, 4418–4424.
  • Compounds: 1 = 2,4-dimethoxy-1-tritiobenzene [3H]c1ccc(OC)cc1OC; 2 = 2,4,6-trimethoxy-1-tritiobenzene [3H]c1c(OC)cc(OC)cc1OC. Stable in aqueous base; prolonged aqueous acid → lose T, giving 1,3-dimethoxybenzene and 1,3,5-trimethoxybenzene.
  • Asked: (a) mechanism of T loss; (b) which loses T faster and why.
  • Answer [H]: (a) acid-catalyzed electrophilic aromatic hydrogen exchange (protodetritiation): H3O⁺ protonates the ring carbon bearing T → arenium ion (σ complex, stabilized by OMe groups ortho/para) → loss of T⁺ restores aromaticity. (b) 2 faster: the protonated carbon is ortho/para to three OMe groups (two ortho, one para) vs two (one ortho, one para) in 1 → more stabilized arenium ion/TS.

19.87 — book p.902 (도전 문제) — footnote 5

  • Citation: Prog. Stereochem. 1958, 2, 125.
  • Compound: 2-iodo-1,3,5-trinitrobenzene (picryl iodide) Ic1c([N+](=O)[O-])cc([N+](=O)[O-])cc1[N+](=O)[O-]. Bond a = C–NO2 ortho to I: 1.45 Å; bond b = C–NO2 para to I: 1.35 Å.
  • Answer [H]: steric inhibition of resonance — the large iodine forces the two ortho nitro groups to twist out of the ring plane, so no π overlap with the ring (pure single C–N bond, longer); the para nitro group is coplanar and conjugated (quinoid resonance gives partial C=N double-bond character → shorter).

19.88 — book p.902 — footnote 6

  • Citation: Org. Lett. 1999, 1, 985–988.
  • Context: synthesis of antitumor 7-hydroxynitidine (benzo[c]phenanthridine).
  • Reaction: 1 = N-(5-bromo-2-benzyloxy-3,4-dimethoxybenzyl)-amine of 6,7-methylenedioxy-1-naphthylamine (aryl bromide with ArCH2–NH–naphthyl) + NaNH2 (2 equiv), then H2O → 2 = dihydrobenzo[c]phenanthridine (new aryl–aryl bond between benzyl ring and naphthalene C2, N–H retained; OCH2Ph, two OMe, methylenedioxy).
  • Answer [H]: 1st equiv NaNH2 deprotonates N–H → anilide anion (delocalized onto naphthalene C2, enamine-like carbanion character); 2nd equiv removes the aryl H ortho to Br → aryl anion expels Br⁻ → benzyne (between former C–Br and adjacent C–H). Intramolecular nucleophilic attack of the naphthyl C2 (ortho to N⁻) on the proximal benzyne carbon forms the 6-membered ring (aryne cyclization) → aryl anion; H2O protonates; tautomerization/rearomatization of naphthalene (loss of H from C2) gives 2.

19.89 — book p.902 — footnote 7

  • Citation: J. Org. Chem. 2005, 70, 3511–3517.
  • Reaction: 3'-methoxy-2-(phenylethynyl)biphenyl COc1cccc(-c2ccccc2C#Cc2ccccc2)c1 + ICl (I⁺ source) → 9-iodo-10-phenylphenanthrenes A (OMe para to new C–C bond; Ic1c(-c2ccccc2)c2ccc(OC)cc2c2ccccc12) + B (OMe ortho to new C–C bond, peri to C10-Ph; Ic1c(-c2ccccc2)c2c(OC)cccc2c2ccccc12), A:B ≈ 3:1.
  • Asked: (a) mechanisms; (b) why A favored.
  • Answer [H]: I⁺ activates alkyne (iodonium) → methoxyphenyl ring attacks the Ph-bearing alkyne carbon (6-endo intramolecular SEAr, vinyl-cation-like), either at C para to OMe (→ A) or C ortho to OMe (between OMe and the biaryl bond, → B); deprotonation restores aromaticity. (b) Both positions are activated by OMe (para/ortho), but the ortho site is sterically hindered (flanked by OMe; product B has OMe/phenyl peri-type clash), so para attack (A) dominates.

19.90 — book p.902 — footnote 8

  • Citation: J. Org. Chem. 1999, 64, 6702–6705.
  • Reaction: quinuclidin-3-one (1) O=C1CN2CCC1CC2 + benzene, CF3SO3H (pKa ≈ −14) → 3,3-diphenylquinuclidinium (3) [NH+]12CCC(CC1)C(c1ccccc1)(c1ccccc1)C2, via superelectrophilic dication 2 (N-protonated + O-protonated).
  • Answer [H]: TfOH protonates N then O → dication 2 (superelectrophile, carbocation adjacent to ammonium); benzene attacks carbonyl carbon (SEAr, arenium ion, deprotonation) → 3-hydroxy-3-phenylquinuclidinium; OH protonated, loss of H2O → benzylic tertiary carbocation (dication); second benzene attacks (SEAr), deprotonation → 3.

19.91 — book p.902–903 — footnote 9

  • Citation: J. Am. Chem. Soc. 2011, 133, 18614–18617.
  • Reaction: bis(biphenyl-2-yl)amine (1) c1ccc(-c2ccccc2Nc2ccccc2-c2ccccc2)cc1 + BuLi, then BCl3 → N–BCl2 aminoborane (2); AlCl3 → 3 = B–N-doped dibenzo[g,p]chrysene analog (BN replaces one central C=C) B12c3ccccc3-c3ccccc3N1c1ccccc1-c1ccccc12 (C24H16BN).
  • Asked: (a) mechanism 1→3; (b) are all six-membered rings aromatic (extended aromatic system)? (c) X-ray shows central two rings not planar, slightly twisted — why?
  • Answer [M–H]: (a) BuLi deprotonates N–H; amide N attacks BCl3, Cl⁻ lost → 2. AlCl3 abstracts Cl⁻ → borenium cation (strong electrophile) → intramolecular electrophilic borylation (SEAr) at ortho carbon of a pendant phenyl → arenium → deprotonation (HCl); repeat for second Cl/phenyl (double intramolecular Friedel–Crafts-type borylation). (b) Yes: each BN-containing ring has 6 π electrons (N lone pair 2 + two fused benzo C=C 4; B contributes empty p) — 1,2-azaborine-type, isoelectronic with benzene; molecule is isoelectronic/isosteric to dibenzo[g,p]chrysene. (c) Steric repulsion between the H atoms in the fjord/bay regions of the four peripheral benzo rings forces a twist (as in the all-carbon dibenzo[g,p]chrysene).

19.92 — book p.903 — footnote 10

  • Citation: Org. Lett. 2004, 6, 2381–2383.
  • Reaction: bis(2'-bromo-5'-methoxybiphenyl-2-yl) ketone (1) + catalytic aqueous acid (H3O⁺) → 4,4'-dibromo-1,1'-dimethoxy-9,9'-spirobifluorene (Br and OMe positions as drawn: OMe adjacent to spiro-fused ring carbon, Br para to OMe).
  • Answer [H]: protonation of C=O → carbocation (very electrophilic, can effect cyclization spontaneously); intramolecular SEAr by a methoxy-activated aryl ring (attack ortho to OMe) → fluoren-9-ol (9-aryl) after deprotonation; protonation of OH, loss of H2O → 9-arylfluorenyl cation; second intramolecular SEAr by the other methoxy-aryl ring → spiro center, deprotonation → spirobifluorene. Double intramolecular Friedel–Crafts.

  • Ch.15 fn5 and Ch.18 fn2 are the same printed citation (J. Am. Chem. Soc. 1959, 81, 4671–4673) — both azide/tetrazole valence-tautomerism problems.

Chapter 20 — 알데하이드와 케톤 (Aldehydes and Ketones)

PDF pp. 1–10 = book pp. 948–957. (Chapter body not included; only summary + problems.)

Key reactions (반응의 복습, book p. 948)

  1. Hydrate formation (H2O, [H+]) 2. Acetal formation (ROH, [H+], −H2O) 3. Cyclic acetal (HOCH2CH2OH) 4. Cyclic thioacetal (HSCH2CH2SH) 5. Desulfurization (Raney Ni → CH2) 6. Imine (RNH2) 7. Enamine (R2NH) 8. Oxime (NH2OH) 9. Hydrazone (NH2NH2) 10. Wolff–Kishner reduction (NaOH, H2O, heat) 11. Ketone reduction (LiAlH4; H2O) 12. Grignard (RMgBr; H2O) 13. Cyanohydrin (HCN, KCN) 14. Wittig (H2C=PPh3) 15. Baeyer–Villiger (RCO3H). Summary text also stresses: nucleophilic addition (attack, then proton transfer); hemiacetal→acetal; acetals as protecting groups (stable to base); unstabilized ylides → (Z)-alkenes, stabilized → (E); B–V migratory aptitude; IR C=O ~1715 cm⁻¹, aldehyde C–H 2700/2850; ¹H CHO ~10 ppm; ¹³C C=O ~200 ppm.
